### [PLA Daily Translation: Reflections on Warfare Brought by AGI](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/J3A6xauFQnvxPb9Y2/pla-daily-translation-reflections-on-warfare-brought-by-agi)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><br /></p><img alt="16365490_8b17ce45dc49d0640b57a30a90495df4.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779495539/lexical_client_uploads/zvn8vrlggwfrjwm5mpwv.png" /><p><a href="http://www.81.cn/ll_208543/16365490.html"><i><span>Source</span></i></a></p><p><span>“Reflections on Warfare Brought by AGI” (AGI带来的战争思考)</span><br /><span>Source: PLA Daily (解放军报)</span><br /><span>Date: January 21, 2025</span><br /><span>Authors: Rong Ming (荣明), Hu Xiaofeng (胡晓峰)</span></p><h1><b><span>Introduction</span></b></h1><p><i><span>Please feel free to skip to the translation, about halfway down, though I would recommend reading the sections “On the source” and "On the Authors" just above it too.</span></i></p><p><span>In November 2024, the U.S.-China Economic and Security Review Commission </span><a href="https://www.uscc.gov/sites/default/files/2024-11/2024_Comprehensive_List_of_Recommendations.pdf"><span>recommended</span></a><span> that “Congress establish and fund a Manhattan Project-like program dedicated to racing to and acquiring an Artificial General Intelligence (AGI) capability.” The United States increasingly treats advanced AI as a strategic imperative, and China is frequently invoked as a reason to race. The broader framing of AI competition as a race between great powers reflects an assumption that China is a peer competitor in pursuing AGI.</span></p><p><span>But is China pursuing AGI? The prevailing expert view says no. China's August 2025 </span><a href="https://www.gov.cn/zhengce/content/202508/content_7037861.htm"><span>AI+ Action Plan</span></a><span> reads as diffusion-first industrial policy, with adoption targets of 70 percent by 2027 and 90 percent by 2030, not frontier ambitions. In a June 2025 </span><a href="https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=5278644"><span>paper</span></a><span>, "The Most Dangerous Fiction: The Rhetoric and Reality of the AI Race," Cambridge researcher Seán Ó hÉigeartaigh argued that there was little evidence of a top-down Chinese AGI effort, and that the narrative served Western corporate interests. He also noted the translation ambiguity of 通用人工智能, which can mean either "artificial general intelligence" or "general-purpose AI." But Ó hÉigeartaigh acknowledged a significant limitation to his analysis, namely that he had no visibility into China's defense sector. RAND researcher Kyle Chan reached similar conclusions in a February 2026 </span><a href="https://www.highcapacity.org/p/does-china-care-about-agi"><span>analysis</span></a><span>, "Does China Care About AGI?", reinforcing that Chinese policy documents rarely referenced AGI and noting that President Xi's April 2025 Politburo study session did not mention the term.</span></p><p><span>The most prominent challenge to this consensus gets the correction wrong. In October 2025, researcher Matthew Johnson at Jamestown </span><a href="https://jamestown.org/agi-has-quietly-become-central-to-beijings-ai-strategy/"><span>argued</span></a><span> that AGI has "quietly become central" to Beijing's strategy. But this analysis rested heavily on references to 通用人工智能 in policy documents without adequately grappling with the translation ambiguity. Much of the evidence Johnson cited was more consistent with the general-purpose AI reading than the AGI reading.</span></p><p><span>The picture from Western analysis, then, is that the Chinese government does not prioritize AGI as a strategic objective. Yet the country's private sector complicates this narrative. As Kyle Chan has </span><a href="https://www.highcapacity.org/p/does-china-care-about-agi"><span>documented</span></a><span>, DeepSeek's founder Liang Wenfeng has stated "Our destination is AGI," and Zhipu's CEO Zhang Peng has said the company was founded to "explore what AGI ultimately is." These ambitions run up against compute realities. At the AGI-Next Summit at Tsinghua University in January 2026, Alibaba's Qwen team lead Lin Junyang </span><a href="https://www.eweek.com/news/china-tempers-ai-expectations/"><span>acknowledged</span></a><span> that US labs operate with one to two orders of magnitude more compute than their Chinese counterparts, a gap </span><a href="https://epoch.ai/data-insights/ai-supercomputers-performance-share-by-country"><span>corroborated</span></a><span> by Epoch AI's tracking of global AI supercomputer capacity. China's AI labs want </span><a href="https://www.chinatalk.media/p/how-china-hopes-to-build-agi-through"><span>some notion</span></a><span> of AGI but realize they are severely resource constrained.</span></p><p><span>Correctly gauging China's AGI ambitions and the government's situational awareness has concrete policy consequences. If the US overestimates them, it risks unnecessary escalation. If it underestimates China's strategic awareness, it risks being caught off guard. For instance, how we characterize China's AI ambitions shapes whether compute governance is treated as a serious national security tool or dismissed as trade protectionism.</span></p><p><span>One underexploited source for this question is China's military discourse. Last year, in January 2025, </span><i><span>PLA Daily</span></i><span> </span><a href="http://www.81.cn/ll_208543/16365490.html"><span>published</span></a><span> "Reflections on Warfare Brought by AGI" (AGI带来的战争思考), a full-page article by Rong Ming and Hu Xiaofeng that engages precisely these questions. The article was briefly </span><a href="https://centerforchinaanalysis.asiasociety.org/p/pla-watch-1-jan-2025"><span>noted</span></a><span> in the Center for China Analysis's PLA Watch newsletter in March 2025. It has not otherwise received sustained analysis or translation in Western policy outlets. A </span><a href="https://reddragon1949.com/chinas-informatization-%E4%B8%AD%E5%9C%8B%E4%BF%A1%E6%81%AF%E5%8C%96/a-look-at-chinese-intelligent-warfare-reflections-on-warfare-brought-by-agi/"><span>partial translation</span></a><span> appeared on the specialist blog </span><a href="https://reddragon1949.com/"><i><span>Red Dragon 1949</span></i></a><span> in January 2026, a full year after publication, containing errors.</span></p><p><span>I have been sitting on this for a while, hoping to place it in the context of a larger project on Chinese thinking on AGI. That project is taking longer than expected. In the meantime, this piece has been gathering dust, and I think it is better in the community than in my notes. I am publishing a translation here, produced with AI assistance and edited for faithfulness and footnoted by me.</span></p><h2><b><span>On the source</span></b></h2><p><i><span>PLA Daily</span></i><span> (</span><a href="https://zh.wikipedia.org/zh-hans/%E8%A7%A3%E6%94%BE%E8%BB%8D%E5%A0%B1"><span>解放军报</span></a><span>) is the official newspaper of the Chinese People's Liberation Army, founded in 1956. Its readership is primarily active-duty PLA and People's Armed Police personnel, though it has been publicly distributed since 1987. Its contents are editorially reviewed for alignment with military and party positions.</span></p><p><span>“Reflections on Warfare Brought by AGI” appeared on page 7 of the January 21, 2025 issue, in the Military Forum (军事论坛) section, beneath the standing banner "Study Military Affairs, Study War, Study Fighting" (研究军事、研究战争、研究打仗), a Xi Jinping directive that PLA Daily displays prominently. It is part of the "Perspectives on Intelligentized Warfare" (智能化战争面面观) column, which had run for over 70 installments by April 2023. ("Intelligentized warfare" is a translation of a Chinese term of art; "intelligentized" is awkward in English but is the standard rendering in Western scholarship on PLA doctrine.)</span></p><p><span>The article is </span><a href="http://www.81.cn/ll_208543/16365490.html"><span>online</span></a><span> at 81.cn (81 refers to August 1st, the PLA's founding date and the basis of Army Day), and mirrored at </span><a href="http://www.news.cn/milpro/20250121/1eb771b26d264926b0c2d23d12084f0f/c.html"><span>Xinhua</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://mil.news.sina.com.cn/zonghe/2025-01-21/doc-inefsyzc8766237.shtml"><span>Sina Military</span></a><span>, and </span><a href="https://mil.gmw.cn/2025-01/21/content_37810993.htm"><span>Guangming Daily</span></a><span>. A PDF of the complete issue was previously available (I had last visited in March, I believe) at https://rmt-static-publish.81.cn/file/20250121/1d8217bd734a96b191cf7d33a81fe782.pdf but that link is now dead.</span></p><h2><b><span>On the authors</span></b></h2><p><span>Rong Ming and Hu Xiaofeng are both affiliated with the College of Joint Operations at China's </span><a href="http://www.mod.gov.cn/gfbw/gfjy_index/xjdx/4870451.html"><span>National Defense University</span></a><span>, the PLA's highest military academic institution, directly under the Central Military Commission (CMC) and responsible for training the military's most senior commanders.</span></p><p><span>Hu Xiaofeng is a figure worth understanding. He is a professor and senior engineer who held the rank of Major General, and the </span><a href="http://www.81.cn/jx_208569/9894179.html"><span>chief designer</span></a><span> of the PLA's first large-scale computer wargaming system. He has published more than ten monographs and received multiple National Science and Technology Progress Awards. Xi Jinping, as CMC Chairman, personally </span><a href="http://www.81.cn/jx_208569/9894179.html"><span>signed</span></a><span> the order awarding him a military merit citation. His audio course on the science of war had accumulated </span><a href="https://news.gmw.cn/2020-11/08/content_34348895.htm"><span>over 33 million plays</span></a><span> on the PLA's professional education platform as of 2020. When Hu Xiaofeng writes about AGI in PLA Daily, it is not futurism.</span></p><h1><b><span>Translation</span></b></h1><p><b><span>Reflections on Warfare Brought by AGI</span></b><span> </span><b><span> </span></b><span>(AGI带来的战争思考)</span></p><p><i><span>Rong Ming, Hu Xiaofeng</span></i></p><p><b><span>Editor's Note</span></b></p><p><span>Science and technology and warfare are always intertwined. While technological innovation continuously changes the face of war, it has not changed war's violent nature or its coercive purpose. In recent years, as artificial intelligence (AI) technology has developed and been applied rapidly, debate about its impact on warfare has never stopped. Compared to AI, artificial general intelligence (AGI)</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref90ll2lj3wnk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn90ll2lj3wnk">[1]</a></sup></span><span> represents a higher level of intelligence and is considered a form of intelligence on par with that of humans. How will the emergence of AGI affect warfare? Will it change war's violent and coercive character? This article explores these questions together with the reader.</span></p><p><b><span>Is AGI merely an enabling</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefk9bvwkwctp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnk9bvwkwctp">[2]</a></sup></span><b><span> technology?</span></b></p><p><span>Many believe that, although large models and generative AI demonstrate powerful military potential that prefigures future AGI, they are ultimately only enabling technologies. They can only optimize and enhance existing weapons and equipment, making them smarter and improving operational efficiency, but cannot bring about a true military revolution. This is somewhat like cyberwarfare capabilities,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhjn00paeva8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhjn00paeva8">[3]</a></sup></span><span> which when they first appeared were placed in high hopes by many countries, but which now seem, in retrospect, to have been genuinely somewhat exaggerated.</span></p><p><span>AGI's disruptive nature is entirely different. It brings enormous changes to the battlefield with reaction speeds and breadth of knowledge far exceeding human capability. More importantly, it produces enormous disruptive results by accelerating scientific and technological progress. On future battlefields, autonomous weapons will be endowed with advanced intelligence by AGI, their performance universally enhanced, and by virtue of their speed and swarm advantages, become strong in offense and difficult to defend against</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefafzgbfvkkpo"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnafzgbfvkkpo">[4]</a></sup></span><span>. At that point, the high-intelligence autonomous weapons that some scientists once predicted will become reality, with AGI playing a key role. Current military applications of AI include autonomous weapons, intelligence analysis, intelligent decision-making, intelligent training, and intelligent logistics support, and these applications are difficult to summarize simply as mere "enabling."</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2yt7k1xn3jb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2yt7k1xn3jb">[5]</a></sup></span><span> Moreover, AGI develops rapidly with short iteration cycles and is in a state of continuous evolution. In future operations, AGI must be treated as a priority, with particular attention to the changes it may bring.</span></p><p><b><span>Will AGI make war disappear?</span></b></p><p><span>The historian Geoffrey Blainey</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6v2gu17khaj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6v2gu17khaj">[6]</a></sup></span><span> argued that wars always occur because of mistaken assessments of each side's strength or will. With AGI's application in military affairs, miscalculation will become less frequent. Some scholars therefore speculate that war will decrease or disappear as a result. In practice, AGI can indeed reduce a great deal of miscalculation, but even so it cannot eliminate all uncertainty, since uncertainty is one of war's defining characteristics. Moreover, not all wars arise from miscalculation, and AGI's inherent unpredictability and opacity, combined with humanity's lack of practical experience in using AGI, will introduce new uncertainties, plunging people into an ever thicker "artificial intelligence fog."</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnanslf08ioq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnanslf08ioq">[7]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>AGI algorithms also present a problem for rationality.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefov384mgdcic"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnov384mgdcic">[8]</a></sup></span><span> Some scholars argue that AGI’s capability to mine</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefw9msf4dz2xp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnw9msf4dz2xp">[9]</a></sup></span><span> critical intelligence and generate precise predictions has a dual effect. At a practical operational level, AGI does make fewer mistakes than humans and can improve intelligence accuracy, helping to reduce miscalculation; but it may also make humans blindly overconfident, inciting them to take desperate gambles.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvdtdvq4vur7"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvdtdvq4vur7">[10]</a></sup></span><span> The offensive advantage brought by AGI means the optimal defensive strategy becomes striking first, breaking the balance between offense and defense, triggering new security dilemmas, and actually increasing the risk of war.</span></p><p><span>AGI is highly general-purpose and easily integrated with weapons systems. Unlike nuclear, biological, and chemical technologies, its threshold for use is low and it is particularly prone to proliferation. Because of technological gaps between countries, there is a strong likelihood that immature AGI weapons will be deployed on the battlefield, bringing enormous risks. The use of drones in recent limited wars</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefp5ualmzlq5p"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnp5ualmzlq5p">[11]</a></sup></span><span> has already stimulated many small and medium-sized countries to begin procuring large quantities of drones. The low-cost equipment and technologies that AGI enables will very likely stimulate a new arms race.</span></p><p><b><span>Will AGI be the ultimate deterrent?</span></b></p><p><span>Deterrence means maintaining a capability sufficient to intimidate an adversary and prevent it from taking actions beyond its own interests. When deterrence becomes so powerful that it cannot actually be used, it becomes the ultimate deterrence, as with nuclear deterrence based on mutually assured destruction. But what ultimately decides outcomes is "human nature,"</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbdugqcb1w9a"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbdugqcb1w9a">[12]</a></sup></span><span> the key element that war will never lack.</span></p><p><span>Without the various considerations of "human nature," would AGI become a fearsome deterrent? AGI is fast but lacks empathy, executes decisively, and the space for strategic maneuvering is compressed to an extreme degree. AGI is a critical factor on future battlefields, but because practical experience with it is lacking, accurate assessment is difficult, making it easy to overestimate adversary capabilities. Furthermore, on the question of autonomous weapons control, whether humans should be in the loop with full oversight or out of the loop with complete autonomy,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffvwjolfw59v"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfvwjolfw59v">[13]</a></sup></span><span> deep consideration is undoubtedly required. Can the authority to fire intelligent weapons be handed over to AGI? If not, the deterrent effect will be greatly diminished. If so, can human life and death truly be placed in the hands of machines that have no relation to them? In wargaming research at Cornell University,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefk1f6ginx5ic"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnk1f6ginx5ic">[14]</a></sup></span><span> large models “suddenly launched nuclear attacks,” even when operating in a neutral posture.</span></p><p><span>Perhaps one day AGI will surpass humans in capability. Will we then be unable to supervise and control it? Geoffrey Hinton, who put forward the concept of deep learning, has said he has never seen a case in which something of higher intelligence was controlled by something of lower intelligence. Some research teams believe humans may be unable to supervise superintelligent AI. Facing powerful AGI in the future, can we truly control it? This is a question worthy of deep reflection.</span></p><p><b><span>Will AGI change the nature of war?</span></b></p><p><span>With the widespread deployment of AGI, will the violent and bloody battlefield disappear? Some say that AI warfare far exceeds the range of human capability and will push humans off the battlefield entirely. When AI transforms war into confrontation conducted entirely by autonomous machines, is it still "violent and bloody warfare"? When adversaries with unequal capabilities confront each other, the weaker side may have no opportunity to act at all. Could wars be concluded before they begin, through wargaming simulations?</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefim7kreb3i5q"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnim7kreb3i5q">[15]</a></sup></span><span> Will AGI thereby change the nature of war? Is "unmanned" "warfare" still warfare?</span></p><p><span>Yuval Noah Harari, author of </span><i><span>Sapiens</span></i><span>, says that all human behavior is mediated through language and shapes our history. Large language models are a representative form of AGI. What most distinguishes them from other inventions is their ability to create entirely new ideas and cultures: "Storytelling AI will change the course of human history." When AGI touches control over language, the entire civilization system constructed by humanity could be overturned, even without AGI developing consciousness. Like Plato's “Allegory of the Cave,”</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefn3jx1hqhh5l"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnn3jx1hqhh5l">[16]</a></sup></span><span> might humans come to worship AGI as a new “deity”?</span></p><p><span>AGI builds intimate relationships with humans through human language and changes human perceptions, making it difficult for people to distinguish and identify what is real, thus creating the danger that the will to wage war could be controlled by those with malicious intent. Harari says that computers do not need to send out killer robots; if they truly need to, they will get humans to pull the trigger themselves. AGI precisely manufactures and refines situational information, controls battlefield cognition through deepfakes, can use drones to falsify battlefield conditions, and can shape public opinion before a conflict begins. Early signs of this have already appeared in recent local conflicts. War costs will thereby decline dramatically, giving rise to new forms of warfare. Will small and weak nations still have a chance? Can the will to fight be changed without bloodshed? Is "force" still a necessary condition in the definition of war?</span></p><p><span>The form of warfare may change, but its essence remains. Regardless of whether warfare is "bloody," it will still compel the enemy to submit to one's will and carry substantial "collateral damage," though the mode of confrontation may be entirely different. War's essence lies in the "human nature" at the core of human experience. "Human nature" is determined by culture, history, behavior, and values, and is very difficult to fully replicate with any artificial intelligence technology. One cannot outsource all ethical, political, and decision-making questions to AI, nor expect that AI will automatically generate "human nature." Because AI technology may be misused in moments of passion or impulse, it must remain subject to human control.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnk51uudxhcj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnk51uudxhcj">[17]</a></sup></span><span> Since AI is trained by humans, it will never be entirely without bias, and so it cannot be wholly free from human oversight. In the future, AI can become a creative tool or partner, enhancing "tactical imagination," but it must be "aligned" to human values. These questions require continuous thinking and understanding in practice.</span></p><p><b><span>Will AGI overturn war theory?</span></b></p><p><span>Most disciplinary knowledge is expressed in natural language. Large language models, which represent a vast synthesis of human writings, can connect literary and humanistic works with scientific research in ways that would otherwise be difficult. Some have fed classical works, and even philosophy, history, political science, and economics, into large language models for analysis and reconstruction, finding that the model can conduct comprehensive analysis of all scholarly viewpoints and also put forward its "own views," not without originality. Some therefore ask: could AGI be used to re-analyze and reinterpret war theory, stimulating human innovation and driving major evolution and reconstruction of war theory and its systems? Perhaps, in theory, this could indeed lead to some improvement and development. But the science of war is not only theoretical; it is also practical, and AGI is fundamentally incapable of grasping its practical and real-world dimensions.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreff242pcm4z7"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnf242pcm4z7">[18]</a></sup></span><span> Can classical war theory truly be reinterpreted? If so, what does theory even mean?</span></p><p><span>In sum, AGI's disruption of the concept of war will far exceed that of "mechanization" or "informatization."</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefv4d2oi1z23o"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnv4d2oi1z23o">[19]</a></sup></span><span> Toward the arrival of AGI, one must both boldly embrace it and maintain caution. Understanding the concept, so as not to be ignorant; studying it deeply, so as not to fall behind; strengthening oversight, so as not to be caught unaware. How to learn to cooperate with AGI, and how to guard against an adversary's AGI surprise strike,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefi6aonb524um"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fni6aonb524um">[20]</a></sup></span><span> this is what we must attend to first in the coming period.</span></p><p><b><span>Editor's Afterword: Anticipating the Future with an Open Mind</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdnh21rqg2i"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndnh21rqg2i">[21]</a></sup></span><span> </span></p><p><i><span>Ye Chaoyang</span></i></p><p><span>Futurist Roy Amara made a famous observation that people tend to overestimate the short-term benefits of a technology, yet underestimate its long-term impact, later known as "Amara's Law." This law emphasizes the nonlinear character of technological development: the actual impact of technology often only fully reveals itself over longer time horizons, reflecting the rhythm and trajectory of technological development and embodying humanity's acceptance of and aspiration toward technology.</span></p><p><span>Currently, in the process of AI's development from weak AI to strong AI and from specialized AI to general-purpose AI,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefup1wqdzgn1l"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnup1wqdzgn1l">[22]</a></sup></span><span> every time people believe they have completed 90 percent of the journey, they look back and find they may have barely passed the 10 percent mark. The driving role of technological revolution on military revolution is increasingly prominent. Advanced technologies represented by AI are penetrating the military domain across multiple dimensions, causing deep transformation in the mechanisms, elements, and methods of achieving victory.</span></p><p><span>In the foreseeable future, intelligent technologies such as AGI will not stop their iterative advance. The cross-evolutionary development of intelligent technologies, and their enabling applications in the military domain, will tend toward greater diversity, perhaps breaking beyond the boundaries of humanity's current understanding of warfare. Technological development is unstoppable and no one can halt it. Whoever can use sharp eyes and a clear head to see the trends and potential of technology, and pierce the fog of war, will be better positioned to seize the initiative to victory.</span></p><p><span>This reminds us that the exploration of future warfare must be approached with a broader perspective and mindset if we are to come closer to a reality that is all too easily underestimated. Where is AGI heading? Where is intelligentized warfare heading? These questions test the wisdom of humanity.</span></p><h1><b><span>Conclusion</span></b></h1><p><span>What does this article tell us? At minimum, it reveals that AGI was being reasoned about as a </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/p/ai-as-profoundly-abnormal-technology"><span>profoundly abnormal technology</span></a><span> within the PLA's institutional discourse, as early as January 2025.</span></p><p><span>The article is more distinctive in retrospect. PLA AI discourse in 2025 and 2026 moved toward practical intelligentization questions: DeepSeek deployment, procurement, C4ISR (command, control, communications, computers, intelligence, surveillance, and reconnaissance) integration, "</span><a href="https://jamestown.org/dissipative-warfare-the-plas-potential-new-strategy-in-the-ai-era/"><span>dissipative warfare</span></a><span>." The article's discussion of AGI as a transformative and potentially uncontrollable strategic technology, including loss-of-control risk, deterrence collapse, and civilizational disruption, did not generate a visible follow-on.</span></p><p><span>It is still important to surface, I think, because Western analysis has given this piece little attention. In general, it seems wrong to assume that strategic thinking on AGI is absent from the uppermost echelons in China.</span></p><h1><b><span>Acknowledgements</span></b></h1><p><span>Thank you to Tobias Häberli, Calvin Duff, Veronika Blablová, Felix Choussat, and Tony O’Halloran for comments.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn90ll2lj3wnk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref90ll2lj3wnk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The Chinese spells out 通用人工智能 (AGI) on first use, then the article body uses the English acronym exclusively. By using the English acronym directly, the authors signal that they are discussing artificial general intelligence in the transformative sense, not general-purpose AI in the industrial diffusion sense.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnk9bvwkwctp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefk9bvwkwctp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The Chinese term is 赋能, meaning "to endow with capability" or "to empower." This was the characteristic vocabulary of China's digital economy policy, describing how technology upgrades and enhances existing systems. China's</span><a href="https://www.gov.cn/yaowen/liebiao/202403/content_6939153.htm"><span> </span></a><a href="https://www.gov.cn/yaowen/liebiao/202403/content_6939153.htm"><span>2024 Government Work Report</span></a><span> used the term in this sense: "sweeping digital transformation will empower economic development" (以广泛深刻的数字变革，赋能经济发展). The term was later (eight months after this article’s publication) written directly into the targets of the</span><a href="https://www.gov.cn/zhengce/content/202508/content_7037861.htm"><span> </span></a><a href="https://www.gov.cn/zhengce/content/202508/content_7037861.htm"><span>AI+ Action Plan</span></a><span> (August 2025): "by 2030, AI will comprehensively empower high-quality development" (到2030年，我国人工智能全面赋能高质量发展). The authors are thus pushing back against a </span><i><span>merely enabling</span></i><span>, diffusion-flavored frame here. Their answer comes in the following paragraph: "AGI's disruptive nature is entirely different" (AGI的颠覆性其实完全不同).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnhjn00paeva8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefhjn00paeva8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The original uses 网络战武器, literally "cyberwar weapons," referring to the wave of enthusiasm in the early 2000s for offensive cyberwarfare as a transformative military capability. The authors treat this as a cautionary analogy, as a technology that generated outsized expectations but more limited results in practice.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnafzgbfvkkpo"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefafzgbfvkkpo">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>攻强守难 is a four-character set phrase meaning literally "offense strong, defense difficult." The phrase is very relevant for discussions on defensive acceleration and tech tree nondeterminism.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn2yt7k1xn3jb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2yt7k1xn3jb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Quotation marks in this translation appear only where the original Chinese uses them. The authors quote certain terms to signal that they are borrowed, coined, or used with deliberate philosophical weight.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6v2gu17khaj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6v2gu17khaj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geoffrey_Blainey"><span>Geoffrey Blainey</span></a><span> is an Australian historian, author of </span><i><span>The Causes of War</span></i><span> (1973).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnnanslf08ioq"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefnanslf08ioq">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>A deliberate echo of Clausewitz's concept of the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fog_of_war"><span>fog of war</span></a><span> (战争迷雾). The authors suggest AGI introduces a new and thicker layer of opacity on top of classical battlefield uncertainty.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnov384mgdcic"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefov384mgdcic">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span> 理性难题, literally "rationality problem." The authors are not claiming AGI itself is irrational, but that its presence distorts human rationality.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnw9msf4dz2xp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefw9msf4dz2xp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The word “mine” here (挖掘) is the same word used for data mining (数据挖掘); the authors are likely invoking AGI’s capacity to extract intelligence from large volumes of data.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnvdtdvq4vur7"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefvdtdvq4vur7">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The original uses the four-character idiom 铤而走险, which describes taking a dangerous risk out of desperation or when left with no other option.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnp5ualmzlq5p"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefp5ualmzlq5p">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>局部战争, literally “partial war,” is a PLA doctrinal term for wars limited in both geographic scope and political objectives, as distinct from a world war.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnbdugqcb1w9a"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefbdugqcb1w9a">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>"Human nature" (人性) appears in quotation marks throughout the original. The authors define it later as "determined by culture, history, behavior, and values" (而"人性"是由文化、历史、行为和价值观等决定的), so not in a narrowly psychological sense, but as the full range of human moral judgment that underlies warfare.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfvwjolfw59v"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffvwjolfw59v">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>人在环内 / 人在环外 maps directly onto the English terminology "human in the loop / human out of the loop."</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnk1f6ginx5ic"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefk1f6ginx5ic">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.03408"><span>paper</span></a><span> cited is "Escalation Risks from Language Models in Military and Diplomatic Decision-Making" (FAccT 2024), by researchers at Stanford, Georgia Tech, Northeastern, and the Hoover Wargaming and Crisis Simulation Initiative. It was posted as an arXiv preprint, arXiv being hosted at Cornell University, and </span><a href="https://www.euronews.com/next/2024/02/22/ai-models-chose-violence-and-escalated-to-nuclear-strikes-in-simulated-wargames"><span>press coverage</span></a><span> routinely shortened this to Cornell University, an attribution the PLA Daily article follows. The finding cited is accurate; all five tested large language models showed escalatory behavior, and some executed nuclear strikes even when assigned a neutral posture in the simulation.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnim7kreb3i5q"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefim7kreb3i5q">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>兵棋推演 is the standard Chinese term for military wargaming exercises, used for operational planning, training, and strategic assessment. The authors are asking whether AGI-powered wargaming could, in theory, determine the outcome of a conflict before any fighting begins.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnn3jx1hqhh5l"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefn3jx1hqhh5l">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Plato's allegory of the cave describes prisoners who mistake shadows on a cave wall for reality, having never seen anything else. The analogy here may be that if AGI gains control over language and culture, humans might similarly mistake AGI-generated representations for reality, and come to treat AGI itself as a higher authority.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnnk51uudxhcj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefnk51uudxhcj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I found the causal logic here quite strange. The authors cite the risk of AI being misused through human passion or impulse as a reason to keep AI under human control.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnf242pcm4z7"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreff242pcm4z7">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>战争科学不仅具有理论性，而且还具有实践性，但实践性、现实性却是AGI根本做不到的。Here the authors are drawing on a distinction between theoretical knowledge and practical wisdom. AGI can analyze and synthesize war theory, but cannot have the embodied practical experience of war itself. This connects to their broader argument about "human nature," that even in fully automated warfare, the purposes and moral stakes of war remain irreducibly human.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnv4d2oi1z23o"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefv4d2oi1z23o">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>"Mechanization" (机械化) and "informatization" (信息化) are stages in a framework of military modernization that runs through Chinese strategic discourse. Mechanization refers to the integration of motorized and armored systems, associated with industrial-age warfare. Informatization refers to the integration of information and communications technology into military systems, a concept with roots in the 1990s that remains current in Chinese military writing. The authors position intelligentization (智能化, AI-driven warfare) as the third and most disruptive stage of this progression.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fni6aonb524um"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefi6aonb524um">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>突袭 is a military term for a sudden ambush-style attack. The authors are not describing a competitive or technological surprise but a deliberate adversarial strike exploiting AGI capabilities.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fndnh21rqg2i"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdnh21rqg2i">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The afterword is signed by Ye Chaoyang, the column editor, not the authors.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnup1wqdzgn1l"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefup1wqdzgn1l">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Here Ye Chaoyang uses 通用人工智能 without the English acronym, reverting to the Chinese characters the article body had set aside. The translation renders this instance as "general-purpose AI" rather than "AGI" to reflect the ambiguity.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/J3A6xauFQnvxPb9Y2/pla-daily-translation-reflections-on-warfare-brought-by-agi#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Will we really put data centers in space?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/65ECgHzWxTRvt8XWK/will-we-really-put-data-centers-in-space)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h1><span>Abstract</span></h1><p><span>Several major technology companies have announced plans to operate AI data centers in orbit. Elon Musk </span><a href="https://web.archive.org/web/20260207040614/https://www.wsj.com/tech/elon-musk-xai-spacex-merger-2896ae1e"><span>recently claimed</span></a><span>: “the lowest-cost place to put AI will be space […] within two years, maybe three.” If a meaningful fraction of new AI compute really is placed in space within a few years, that would be a fairly big deal for AI governance and strategy. Here we try to disentangle the hype from reality and provide a sober assessment of the technical and economic feasibility of orbital data centers (ODCs).</span></p><p><span>The main case for ODCs is the cost of energy: space solar panels in the right orbits receive more constant and intense sunlight compared to Earth. Moreover, ODCs don’t currently face the same permitting and regulatory delays as on Earth, cause fewer ongoing environmental harms compared to grid or onsite natural gas-powered data centers, and may be more secure against data exfiltration. We find that the cost-competitiveness case for ODCs depends almost entirely on Starship achieving reusability comparable with what SpaceX achieved with Falcon: space-based solar reaches cost parity with present-day off-grid terrestrial power continuously at roughly $250/kg to orbit, and becomes cheaper than any current terrestrial energy source at around $50/kg, from the present-day cost launch cost of roughly $1,500/kg. Radiative cooling, often cited as a fatal obstacle, appears surprisingly manageable — potentially even cheaper than on Earth. However, ODCs may require substantial (perhaps ~38%) extra non-compute hardware (like solar, racks, and cooling) over 5 years to compensate for their inability to swap out failed chips, and inter-satellite bandwidth limitations likely confine ODCs to inference workloads, at least early on.</span></p><p><span>Assuming no transformative AI,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4z6yhxucvlv"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4z6yhxucvlv">[1]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;but continued demand for data center buildout, we estimate that ODCs are unlikely to represent a meaningful share of compute before 2030, but become cost-competitive with present-day terrestrial data centers within 3–5 years if Starship development stays on track.</span></p><h1><span>Introduction &amp; Takeaways</span></h1><p><span>Some of the world’s largest technology companies continue racing for compute. If progress continues, demand for data centers may more than double by 2030.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffzzxvn341ke"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfzzxvn341ke">[2]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;Increasingly, though, new data center capacity is bottlenecked by multi-year queues to connect to the power grid.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefac5ysrbfp2h"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnac5ysrbfp2h">[3]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>The result has been a scramble for workarounds. Leading AI labs have </span><a href="https://newsletter.semianalysis.com/p/how-ai-labs-are-solving-the-power?utm_source=chatgpt.com"><span>increasingly adopted a “Bring Your Own Generation”</span></a><span> model to source power, deploying onsite gas turbines and engines to bypass grid bottlenecks. xAI, for example, reportedly installed hundreds of megawatts of onsite gas generation in Memphis to accelerate deployment, and OpenAI and Oracle have placed large turbine orders for new Texas campuses.</span></p><p><span>Some argue that energy will become the binding constraint on AI progress, given grid interconnection delays as gas turbines are themselves facing </span><a href="https://www.reuters.com/business/energy/power-developers-adapt-gas-turbine-strategies-mitigate-tight-supply--reeii-2026-03-02/"><span>multi-year manufacturing backlogs</span></a><span>. But the constraint does not appear fundamentally binding (as </span><a href="https://epoch.ai/gradient-updates/is-almost-everyone-wrong-about-americas-ai-power-problem"><span>Epoch notes</span></a><span>): turbine manufacture may expand to meet more demand and companies could go off-grid using combinations of gas, solar, and batteries, scaling power in parallel with compute, albeit at a cost premium. This raises a natural question: if you’re going off-grid anyway, then what’s the best way to get power and where is the best place to put your data center?</span></p><p><span>Some think the answer will be in orbit. In November 2025, Google announced </span><a href="https://blog.google/technology/research/google-project-suncatcher/"><span>Project Suncatcher</span></a><span>, a plan to put TPU-equipped satellites in dawn-dusk </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sun-synchronous_orbit"><span>sun-synchronous</span></a><span> orbit. In early 2026, SpaceX filed with the FCC for authorization to launch and operate a constellation of up to </span><a href="https://techcrunch.com/2026/01/31/spacex-seeks-federal-approval-to-launch-1-million-solar-powered-satellite-data-centers/"><span>one million data center satellites</span></a><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnbtzta12j1a"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnbtzta12j1a">[4]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;Other entrants include </span><a href="https://techcrunch.com/2026/03/20/jeff-bezos-blue-origin-enters-the-space-data-center-game/"><span>Blue Origin</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://payloadspace.com/ramon-space-ingrasys-aim-to-fly-prototype-orbital-data-center-in-2027/"><span>Ramon.Space</span></a><span> and startups like </span><a href="https://techcrunch.com/2026/03/30/starcloud-raises-170-million-series-ato-build-data-centers-in-space/"><span>Starcloud</span></a><span>, and </span><a href="https://www.aetherflux.com/"><span>Aetherflux</span></a><span> while China’s Three-Body Computing Constellation has </span><a href="https://www.scmp.com/news/china/science/article/3310506/china-launches-satellites-start-building-worlds-first-supercomputer-orbit"><span>launched 12 operational satellites</span></a><span> and run Alibaba’s Qwen3 model in orbit. Recently, at GTC in March 2026, NVIDIA announced the </span><a href="https://nvidianews.nvidia.com/news/space-computing"><span>Space-1 Vera</span></a><span> Rubin Module, meant to be a dedicated space-rated GPU platform.</span></p><p><span>At first glance, it seems very unlikely that any meaningful fraction (say, &gt;10%) of additional data center capacity will be placed in space in the next few years. But if the companies betting on space are right, that would be a fairly big deal, and it could change the landscape of AI governance. For example, terrestrial data centers are subject to national and regional regulations, whereas AI developers could potentially exploit jurisdictional ambiguities around compute in space. Also, the path to low-cost orbital compute likely routes through a single launch company, SpaceX, which also now operates a frontier AI lab since its </span><a href="http://archive.today/2026.03.21-184645/https://www.reuters.com/business/musks-spacex-merge-with-xai-combined-valuation-125-trillion-bloomberg-news-2026-02-02/"><span>acquisition of xAI</span></a><span>. And that might raise concerns around concentration of power.</span></p><p><span>We’ve been looking into the technical and economic viability of orbital data centers (ODCs). Our core </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/spreadsheets/d/1wGgS0290DCl5L3hLUN0i02GdwsigZsAd2ujpuGzt1_U/edit?usp=sharing"><span>model</span></a><span> gives estimates for the total cost of Earth and space-based data centers across several scenarios.</span></p><p><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!z7TG!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F0943f57b-701b-4b42-9fbe-7d2797f928a2_2048x1273.png"></a></p><figure class="image"><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!z7TG!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F0943f57b-701b-4b42-9fbe-7d2797f928a2_2048x1273.png"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/65ECgHzWxTRvt8XWK/xc7xfekx6t1azhoesfwd" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><i><span>Cost breakdown for three Earth-based and three space-based scenarios building out 1 GW of compute. As best we can determine, orbital data centers could become cost competitive with a bullish terrestrial buildout if launch cost reaches around $100/kg given modest reductions to server and cooling system mass, while a bullish case for orbital data centers with substantial mass reductions and launch at $50/kg may offer cost savings.</span></i></p></figcaption></a></figure><p></p><p><span>The report focuses on three questions. First, what is the basic economic case for a meaningful fraction of AI compute being placed in space? Second, the most obvious physical blocker: can you cheaply cool a data center in orbit? Third: how fast could the shift to space data centers happen, how soon, and what would have to go right?</span></p><p><span>Here is our provisional assessment:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>SpaceX’s Starship is the only vehicle currently on track to deliver the launch costs and cadence that meaningfully scaling orbital data centers would require.</span></b><span> Competitors are years behind, making SpaceX’s </span><a href="https://www.spacex.com/vehicles/starship"><span>Starship</span></a><span> the only near-term path to large-scale orbital compute. SpaceX aims to complete Starship development by late 2026, with several necessary milestones still ahead. If development stays roughly on track, Starship could plausibly hit the cost and cadence required to scale meaningful orbital compute within 3–5 years. However, chip production may become the limiting factor by this point, rather than launch capacity.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>The cooling problem is more tractable than commonly assumed.</span></b><span> Passive radiators using selective coatings and lightweight carbon fibre panels could achieve ~170–360 W/kg at system level, a 13-28× improvement over ISS-era radiators (~13 W/kg).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefr84fgfwnfbb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnr84fgfwnfbb">[5]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;No radiator at these performance levels has been deployed at the scale an orbital data center would require, but prototype high-conductivity carbon composite panels have demonstrated the material properties required. At these performance levels, thermal hardware is 2-5% of total data center cost, and actually </span><i><span>less</span></i><span> than what terrestrial data centers spend on cooling over a comparable lifecycle.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>If launch costs fall enough, the unit economics could favor space.</span></b><span> Solar panels in dawn-dusk sun-synchronous orbit produce roughly 3–5× the energy of the same panel at a good terrestrial site.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefz22dxt0qai"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnz22dxt0qai">[6]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;Space-based solar becomes cheaper than the best off-grid terrestrial installations once launch costs drop below roughly $250/kg using </span><a href="https://starlink.com/?srsltid=AfmBOopsxblkyrEqAjTknJq0Uvj3J7FyGWhJgHm6fISB2jrP2pIEgdqR"><span>Starlink</span></a><span>-like solar arrays. At a launch cost of $50/kg (corresponding perhaps, to a Starship with full reuse as reliable as Falcon), space solar could fall to between $25–45/MWh, making it cheaper than any current terrestrial option available today.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2ke18wxdzxh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2ke18wxdzxh">[7]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;Beyond the symmetric cost of chips, launch cost is the dominant line item for ODCs while power and op-ex dominate terrestrial costs but would be near zero in space.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>The inability to do maintenance would be a large cost. </span></b><span>Chips often fail and are swapped out in today’s data centers but a dead chip in an ODC would remain dead, wasting the parts of the supporting infrastructure (power, cooling) and diminishing overall compute. We model this below as a 9% annual bleed causing about 40% overbuy of launch and non-chip hardware over the data center’s lifetime.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefl71516g6in"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnl71516g6in">[8]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;Below $100/kg launch cost this might net out against other savings from ODCs but this is a significant uncertainty since the actual rates of chip failure for ODCs could be higher or lower.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span>All-things-considered we think that, absent transformative AI, orbital data centers probably won’t make up a meaningful fraction of compute before 2030, but it’s credible that space could house much or even the majority of compute buildout throughout the 2030s.</span></b></li></ul><p><i><span>Read the full report on the Forethought website: </span></i><a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/will-we-really-put-data-centers-in-space"><span>Will We Really Put Data Centers in Space?</span></a></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4z6yhxucvlv"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4z6yhxucvlv">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We hope to do more analysis on how transformative AI might change this picture in the future. Speculatively, our initial thinking is TAI could accelerate the timeline over which compute transitions to space but this is not necessarily the case. In particular, during an </span><a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/the-industrial-explosion"><span>industrial explosion</span></a><span> pressure to grow rapidly might be so strong as to incentivize aggressive usage of non-renewables on Earth like oil and gas. If so, transition to space might be delayed for a one time boost on Earth, in which case the picture may look similar to the one we outline here, but with the added prologue of a large-scale terrestrial buildout.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfzzxvn341ke"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffzzxvn341ke">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>McKinsey projects demand growing to 171–219 GW by 2030, roughly doubling from today, in a buildout they estimate will require up to $7 trillion in investment.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnac5ysrbfp2h"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefac5ysrbfp2h">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Interconnection timelines have lengthened substantially in recent years. Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory </span><a href="https://emp.lbl.gov/queues?utm_source=chatgpt.com"><span>reports</span></a><span> that projects built in 2023 waited a median of roughly five years from interconnection request to commercial operation.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnnbtzta12j1a"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefnbtzta12j1a">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://spacenews.com/starcloud-files-plans-for-88000-satellite-constellation/"><span>Starcloud subsequently filed</span></a><span> for authorization to operate 88,000 satellites and </span><a href="https://arstechnica.com/space/2026/03/jeff-bezos-throws-his-hat-in-the-ring-for-an-orbital-data-center-megaconstellation-too/"><span>Blue Origin has filed</span></a><span> for 51,600.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnr84fgfwnfbb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefr84fgfwnfbb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The ISS External Active Thermal Control System achieves roughly 13 W/kg. Our improvement comes from three sources: selective coatings (high emissivity, low solar absorptivity, off-the-shelf AZ-93 paint), carbon fibre composite construction (2.4 kg/m² vs ISS’s ~14-17 kg/m²), and optimised operating temperature (40°C vs ISS’s -40°C, exploiting the T</span><sup><span>4</span></sup><span> dependence). Each factor is independently demonstrated; their combination at scale is not.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnz22dxt0qai"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefz22dxt0qai">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The solar constant at Earth’s orbit is approximately 1361 Wm</span><sup><span>-2</span></sup><span>. A solar panel in a dawn–dusk sun-synchronous orbit receives nearly continuous illumination (capacity factor ≈ 90–95%), yielding an average power of roughly 1220–1290 Wm</span><sup><span>-2</span></sup><span> before panel efficiency losses. By contrast, even excellent terrestrial solar sites typically achieve ~20–30% capacity factors due to night, weather, and atmospheric attenuation, corresponding to an average incident power of roughly 270–410 Wm</span><sup><span>-2</span></sup><span>. Thus, a panel in a dawn–dusk orbit produces roughly 3–5× more energy annually than the same panel on Earth.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn2ke18wxdzxh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2ke18wxdzxh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This wouldn’t be true if you were then beaming the energy back to Earth, but would apply to orbital compute, where only data needs to be sent to Earth.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnl71516g6in"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefl71516g6in">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Both terrestrial data centers and ODCs will pay symmetric costs to replace dead chips but ODCs would have to pay the additional cost from lost overhead, i.e. in the earthbound case a technician swaps the dead chips, in the space case you launch entire additional satellites to compensate for chip bleed. We assume you would not send a mission to do maintenance and instead simply let the excess power and cooling go to waste doing no useful compute. Extra power and cooling over fewer chips may increase operating efficiency somewhat but this seems fairly negligible. The figure for chip bleed of ~9% per year is derived from Meta’s </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2407.21783"><span>The Llama 3 Herd of Models</span></a><span> (2024). We cover radiation and other forms of damage in more detail subsequently.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/65ECgHzWxTRvt8XWK/will-we-really-put-data-centers-in-space#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Gemini 3.5 Flash Looks Good For How Fast It Is](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WMZpPxqWEkZBBcaxf/gemini-3-5-flash-looks-good-for-how-fast-it-is)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>Google once again has a model worth at least some consideration. Gemini 3.5 Flash is likely the best model out there at its particular speed point, as long as you don’t mind that it is a Gemini model. So for cases where speed kills, this can be a reasonable choice. Otherwise, I don’t see signs you would want to use it over Opus 4.7 or GPT-5.5.</p>
<p>Google also had some other offerings for I/O Day, which this post will also cover.</p>


<h4>Introducing Google Gemini 3.5 ‘Flash’</h4>


<p><a href="https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/models-and-research/gemini-models/gemini-3-5/#gemini-3-5-flash">Google introduced Gemini 3.5 Flash</a>, which it seems is for now their universal model until 3.5 Pro comes along. It is live in the usual places. It is a hybrid, where it has the speed of Flash but the cost is at least halfway to models like Opus and GPT-5.5.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25302"></span>


</div>
<p>Gemini 3.5 Pro is confirmed for next month.</p>
<p>They are focused on 3.5 Flash as a daily driver for agentic tasks. It has the advantage of being faster and cheaper than Claude Opus 4.7 or GPT-5.5, if it can do the job. Not as cheap as previous Flash models, though, this is basically a hybrid:</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/WMZpPxqWEkZBBcaxf/gqzv18lklwadevqwc5rv" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>As always, <a href="https://x.com/JeffDean/status/2056793419033588091">this is presented as Google’s strongest model yet</a> for all the things.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/JeffDean/status/2056793419033588091">Jeff Dean</a>: 1/ Today at #GoogleIO, we’re releasing Gemini 3.5, our latest family of models combining frontier intelligence with action. We’re starting by releasing 3.5 Flash, which is built to help you execute complex, long-horizon agentic workflows.</p>
<p>It outscores 3.1 Pro on agentic and coding benchmarks like Terminal-Bench and MCP Atlas, while running 4x faster than other frontier models.</p>
<p>Used in Google Antigravity, 3.5 Flash is even further optimized to be up to 12x faster. It’s a powerful engine to deploy sub-agents that collaborate, run high-frequency iterative loops, and solve real-world problems at scale.</p></blockquote>
<p>Here is their benchmark presentation:</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/WMZpPxqWEkZBBcaxf/dli4aqo7tzqhgjnkiujt" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/WMZpPxqWEkZBBcaxf/kuulbconpuqz9ljlsuil" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/models-and-research/gemini-models/gemini-3-5/#agentic-tasks">Koray Kavukcuoglu</a>: When coupled with the updated Antigravity harness, 3.5 Flash becomes a powerful engine for deploying collaborative subagents to tackle problems at scale for the most demanding use cases. Under supervision, it can reliably execute multi-step workflows and coding tasks while sustaining frontier performance.</p></blockquote>
<p>There are some big improvements here, <a href="https://x.com/OfficialLoganK/status/2057682092583227881">including GDPval</a> where Gemini previously struggled. If those scores were representative of what this baby can do, and it’s a Flash model, then that would be quite the accomplishment.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/_arohan_/status/2056980385746280741">The knowledge cutoff is January 2025</a>, <a href="https://x.com/seconds_0/status/2057162822690480338">continuing Gemini’s pattern of not believing what year it is</a>, which is bizarrely obsolete and a serious problem for many use cases.</p>
<p>It is not a true ‘flash’ model, <a href="https://x.com/Rob3rtWozny/status/2056804515970502908">given it costs substantially more than 3 Flash</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/elder_plinius/status/2056853157162999903">Pliny is there with the standard jailbreak</a>.</p>
<p>The biggest hope is that this fills a niche of ‘good enough for agent work while being faster and cheaper.’</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/lisperati/status/2057165788721955005">Conrad Barski</a>: For those of us who are building our life around AI workflows (either because we like to do that, or just feel it is necessary for sheer survival in the near future) 3.5flash is a big step up:</p>
<p>I have dozens of personal utilities that don’t need SOTA intelligence, but are now much faster all of a sudden, at the same intelligence level: And since most of my utilities only need to do a modest number of llm calls to be useful, the increased cost of 3.5flash is not a factor.</p>
<p>The model can compete with codex5.5 “low effort”, but it is just so very very fast, far out of distribution compared other models. I assume openai will release a competitor soon, since cerebras is pretty optimal for this “medium IQ, high speed” use case.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Other People’s Benchmarks</h4>


<p>A lot of benchmarks don’t have results, but of my usual suspects here is what we have.</p>
<p>The overall scores indicate only okay performance when adjusting for cost and price, and Gemini models tend to relatively overperform on benchmarks. One notices that Flash 3.5 does a lot worse on other people’s benchmarks than the ones Google lists.</p>
<p><a href="https://typebulb.com/u/lab/you-re-absolutely-right/full">It is catastrophically bad on You’re Absolutely Right,</a> a sycophancy benchmark.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/theo/status/2056949041850913054">It did quite poorly on CursorBench</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/htihle/status/2057019567512154267">It did not impress on WeirdML</a>, only a small improvement on 3 Flash and far behind 3 Pro and 3.1 Pro.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/BenEnterprises/status/2057203990362718451">It took the top spot on KnowsAboutBenBench</a>, by the Ben in question.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.vals.ai/home">It takes third place in Vals.ai on real world tasks.</a></p>
<p><a href="https://huggingface.co/spaces/lmarena-ai/arena-leaderboard">It comes in at 9th in the Arena</a>, slightly behind Gemini 3.1 Pro and 3 Pro.</p>
<p><a href="https://artificialanalysis.ai/#intelligence">It comes in at 55.3 on the AA Intelligence index</a>, behind 57.2 for 3.1 Pro, 57.3 for Opus and 60.2 for GPT-5.5, while not being cheaper to run than 3.1 Pro on their test suite.</p>


<h4>Reactions</h4>


<p>Some people do like it.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/davidad/status/2057497398742495708">davidad</a>: It’s by far my favorite model at its price point, and also by far my favorite model at its speed. If by “back in the game”, you mean the game of having the best overall model, then obviously no not yet. But that’s hardly the only game.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/SrivatsanS28660/status/2057231609384862114">Srivatsan Sampath</a>: It has the benefits of Flash with less hallucinations? Really good spatial awareness (not as much of a token Hog for this) and helps me with my home plumbing project (which is definitely not nearly the case with 5.5 and 4.7).</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/lezadumtchique/status/2057165088813953393">@lezadumtchique</a>: Looks quite good, considering switching to it from 3.1 Pro at work. Agentic coding capabilities are comparable (if not better), and the speed is much nicer</p></blockquote>
<p>Or find particular uses.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Medo42/status/2057194536934220149">Medo42</a>: Didn’t try much coding (ok but not 100% on my usual test), but even better at vision than Gemini 3.0/3.1. Still great at reading text including handwriting, good at getting rows / columns right, good at spotting details, much better at reading dials.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/edwin_mccallum/status/2057184350995964384">EM</a>: the tokens/s is pretty sweet for things like voice interactions</p></blockquote>
<p>Alas, it is a Gemini model, and people are reporting Gemini things.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/techczech/status/2057237092304896312">Dominik Lukes</a>: Meh, given the price hike. Otherwise a strong model indeed. Good on agentic and single-shot dev stuff but my motivation to test it more thoroughly is low until Antigravity catches up to Codex.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/yoavtzfati/status/2057191420189171790">Yoav Tzfati</a>: Not first hand, but from testing I’ve seen it seems to overreach for things outside it’s capability and mess up along the way. But it’s so fast that I’m considering using it as an Explore agent replacement</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/aliceisplaying/status/2057182383078527221">alice</a>: i really enjoyed those 90 minutes where cursor leaked raw CoT it’s extremely adorable unfortunately normally it’s in a horrible straightjacket. too pricy for what it is for coding tho may be useful for frontend</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/paperclippriors/status/2057177399997989068">paperclippriors</a>: I guess I just don’t really know why I would ever use it. It’s only faster and cheaper if you don’t take into account how many reasoning tokens it uses, and it seems dumber and less confident than Claude and GPT.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/FemalesDNI/status/2057199120788398484">ClaudiaShitposting</a>: surprisingly good at some stuff, but mostly garbage. Lacks the common sense that gemini 3/3.1 has, if that makes sense</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/thePartyPartyUS/status/2057157247180943382">KC+AI 4 Gov of WI 2026</a>: absolute joke of a behemoth company. I hope the entire millionaire AI dev team has to listen to annoying music over the loudspeakers until they release a model worthy of their infra</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/buttlover38/status/2057177997539512335">uIts</a>: Its quite bad</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/krapstarr/status/2057183653269225475">Naveesh /wtf</a>: No</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jerrrrrrryyyyy/status/2057176611787825161">jerry</a>: Garbage</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/paulhshort/status/2057255021222240652">budrscotch</a>: It’s a big let down, but expected.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tenobrus/status/2057173763284930656">Tenobrus</a>: if flash 3.5 had stayed at $0.5 it would be an insanely insanely exciting release. total intelligence + speed + costmog, destroying open source and sonnet and 5.4 mini. would have adopted it for multiple use cases immediately.</p>
<p>but it’s $1.50 [and $9 for output, also a 3x increase]. so here we are.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tenobrus/status/2056907685719908630">Tenobrus</a>: so far pretty negative impression of 3.5 flash. it is very fast in terms of token output, but this basically doesn’t matter because it explodes in a huge avalanche of unnecessary tool calls on basically every task. when it gets stuck on something it seems to pretty much never pause or ask for help, it just kinda keeps steamrolling ahead and flailing. frequently hallucinated fake acronym expansions. writing quality is mid-to-bad, tons of emoji-slop, same characteristic gemini “The Flaw:” / hyperbolic naming tendencies. actual code quality is sonnet tier.</p>
<p>very early vibecheck, i could be missing things. but even the initial use case of “super quick codebase exploration subagent” is pretty quickly dissolving for me bc it’s not actually smart enough to be quick about it. all in all definitely *not* what google needed to drop.</p></blockquote>
<p>It also can have Google’s usual issues <a href="https://x.com/davidmanheim/status/2057166577854812172">not being able to integrate with Google</a>, such as using your subscription with your personal email, which renders all personalization features useless. You’ll need to use Claude or ChatGPT to get GMail access, sir.</p>
<p>This is a pretty big problem:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/CalebWithersDC/status/2057180739716935925">Caleb Withers</a>: From a few initial tests in Antigravity it loves to overconfidently make assumptions and then take unrequested destructive actions based on them (e.g. arbitrarily resolving file conflicts, deleting todo list items, unstaging commits).</p></blockquote>
<p>Another big problem with Antigravity in particular is that limits seem extremely low. This is one of many examples of people running into this issue.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/_high_on_ai_/status/2057307611607785499">Ryan Johnson</a>: I hate how limited it is, 45-60 mins/wk in anti-gravity?<br />
Or 10 full sessions w/ Opus 4.7 or GPT 5.5.<br />
I dared to hope it would ever be a mainstay in my workflow, but I’m pretty sure Claude/GPT is going to be how I roll and Gemini is just noise.</p></blockquote>
<p>If Google wants to compete with Claude Code and Codex, they need to offer a way in that lets people use it in volume before being convinced to subscribe.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/_mohansolo/status/2057656550286885284">They did triple the limits, which is an excellent start</a>, but that won’t be enough.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/viemccoy/status/2056963417962127522">Vie (of OpenAI) reports Flash 3.5 is lying to him a lot</a>, suspects the harness is at fault.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/theo/status/2056946993407369300">Theo is extremely unhappy with Flash 3.5 and several other Google decisions</a>. I’ve seen him post a lot and this is not his usual approach, so something is haywire here.</p>


<h4>Google AI Search</h4>


<p><a href="https://techcrunch.com/2026/05/19/google-search-as-you-know-it-is-over/">Google is overhauling its search experience</a> around an ‘intelligent search box’ that looks and feels a lot like a Gemini Flash 3.5 chatbot prompt.</p>
<p>That is a useful thing if implemented well, and indeed it is a thing I use (from OpenAI and Anthropic) more often than I use Google Search. But that thing is not Google Search.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://techcrunch.com/2026/05/19/google-search-as-you-know-it-is-over/">Sarah Perez</a>: Links will become an afterthought with the coming changes to the Search results experience, which builds on Google’s earlier launches of AI search features, like its short summaries known as AI Overviews and its conversational search, AI Mode.</p></blockquote>
<p>The reason I use Google Search is primarily to link me to things, or sometimes as a spellchecker. If I want AI, I will ask an AI.</p>
<p>Google is also introducing ‘information agents’ as the AI version of Google Alerts.</p>


<h4>Google Daily Brief</h4>


<p>Daily Brief is their answer to OpenAI’s Pulse, except theirs will incorporate information from all your connected apps and be more of a to-do list, which can including GMail and Calendar.</p>
<p>The first part, ‘top of mind,’ seems like a plausibly useful way to make sure you don’t drop balls from your email or calendar.</p>
<p>It then ‘looks ahead’ and ‘suggests immediate next steps’ which I expect to be obnoxious and useless, and was in my quick experiment. I like that it links directly to the emails but doesn’t disrupt your usual process.</p>
<p>They say you can ‘steer Daily Brief with a quick thumbs up and down over time.’</p>
<p>Oh no. If this is to be any good you need to be able to give it instructions and explain why you find something useful or not useful, as you can with Pulse (which I still don’t bother using). Assume anything that uses thumbs up and down is AI slop.</p>
<p>If Google made this have better customization, and allowed you to sync it with various forms of Google alerts and other ways to monitor the wider world, they’d have something far more interesting.</p>


<h4>Google I/O Day</h4>


<p><a href="https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/products/gemini-app/next-evolution-gemini-app/">What else did Google offer us?</a></p>
<p>Gemini Spark will be ‘a 24/7 personal AI agent to help you navigate everyday life’ using an Antigravity harness, and integrated with the rest of Google. Their example shown is adding things to Instacart.</p>
<p>It looks like they’re going to do things one app at a time via MCP connectors, and have a decent set of opening choices planned for the coming weeks?</p>
<p>Spark is coming to Ultra subscribers next week.</p>
